[kernel-isolation/EVIDENCE (dimension: kernel-isolation)] K4 -> corrected
  CLAIM: irqaffinity=<list> sets the default affinity for every non-managed IRQ (the boot CPU is always kept in it); isolcpus=managed_irq cannot move the per-CPU NVMe queue interrupts on CPUs 120-127 because their masks contain a single isolated CPU, but those interrupts fire only for I/O submitted from that CPU; writing smp_affinity of a managed IRQ fails with EPERM (not EIO) on v7.1.3.
  REASON: The kernel quotes are exact: irqdesc.c 28-39 'irq_affinity_setup ... cpulist_parse(str, irq_default_affinity); ... cpumask_set_cpu(smp_processor_id(), irq_default_affinity); return 1; } __setup("irqaffinity=", irq_affinity_setup);'; irq/proc.c 143-144 'if (!irq_can_set_affinity_usr(irq) || no_irq_affinity) return -EPERM;'; kernel-parameters.txt 2792-2796 managed_irq text as quoted. The host numbers are exact: 'default_smp_affinity: ffffffff,ffffffff,ffffffff,ffffffff', 'TOTAL irq dirs parsed: 452 with effective affinity touching 120-127: 16' (nvme0q119-126 and nvme1q119-126, one CPU each), '196 0' in the effective_affinity_list distribution. The evidence file never shows that, and a read-only host check shows all 128 bnxt_en IRQ lines with smp_affinity_list 0-127 and effective_affinity_list 0. The inspection's four '0-63' masks belong to IRQ 13 (ACPI GED/pcc-mbox), 21 (PHNX0001:00), 37 (uart-pl011) and 42 (arch_mem_timer).
  CORRECTION: All 128 bnxt_en network IRQ lines (both ports) have smp_affinity_list 0-127 and effective affinity CPU 0; CPU 0 takes every network interrupt (for example TxRx-42, 893,768 since boot) and CPUs 120-127 take none. irqaffinity=0-125 does not change this; it only guards against future devices.

[kernel-isolation/EVIDENCE (dimension: kernel-isolation)] K10 -> corrected
  CLAIM: isolcpus=domain on all of CPUs 120-127 would make Jeremy's non-pass threads (affinity 120-126 as a group) pile onto one CPU and stay there, because without scheduler domains a new or woken task is placed on its previous or parent's CPU and the SDK pins only the pass thread; isolating only the pass-thread CPU(s) keeps today's balancing over the worker CPUs and still gives the pass thread a domain-isolated core.
  REASON: fair.c 8829-8891: select_task_rq_fair has 'int new_cpu = prev_cpu;' (8834), 'for_each_domain(cpu, tmp) {' (8860), 'if (unlikely(sd)) return sched_balance_find_dst_cpu(sd, p, cpu, prev_cpu, sd_flag);' (8886-8887) and 'return select_idle_sibling(p, prev_cpu, new_cpu);' (8891); select_idle_sibling has 'sd = rcu_dereference_all(per_cpu(sd_llc, target)); if (!sd) return target;' at 8187-8189. kernel-parameters.txt 2762-2763 and cgroup-v2.rst 2657-2659 are verbatim. SDK facts are exact: pass_loop.cpp:36-37 CPU_SET(core) + sched_setaffinity; worker_thread.cpp leave_pass_cpu only clears the pass CPU; churn.py 'others = [c for c in cpus if c not in siblings]' and 'place = None if cpus is None else (lambda: os.sched_setaffinity(0, cpus))'; round4 busy_share_each_watch values 0.0153/0.049/0.1214/0.0829/0.0314/0.026/0.0516/0.7609 are exact; records.py 352 docstring exact. Two corrections: the quote reads rcu_dereference_all, not rcu_dereference; and 'pile onto one CPU and stay there' is a prediction, the code shows only that nothing spreads tasks automatically and that a fork or wake-up keeps the previous or creating CPU. The landing CPU of a worker moved off the pass CPU by leave_pass_cpu is chosen by the affinity-change path, which was not read. Also relevant: after isolcpus=domain, init is moved to the housekeeping mask (sched/core.c 8838-8840), so every process inherits 0-125 unless it sets affinity itself, which Jeremy's 'taskset -c 120-127' does.
  CORRECTION: With isolcpus=domain on 120-127 the scheduler does no load balancing among them and places a new or woken thread on its previous or creating CPU, so the seven non-pass threads would not be spread automatically; where exactly they land is not evidenced. Isolating only the pass-thread CPU(s) keeps today's spread over the worker CPUs.
  SOURCE: scratchpad/kernel-src/v713-fair.c (8187-8189, 8829-8891), v713-sched-core.c (8838-8840), v713-kernel-parameters.txt, v713-cgroup-v2.rst; phoenix-crb-round5.txt (pass_loop.cpp, worker_thread.cpp, churn.py placement, records.py); phoenix-crb-round4.txt '=== churn_r1_job_log ==='

[clock-cpufreq/linux-semantics / clock-cpufreq (cppc_cpufreq, schedutil, performance governor, boost, persistence, AMU feedback counters, WFI wake-up, timer slack, thermal, acceptance rule)] C2 -> corrected
  CLAIM: The live governor change is a root sysfs write per policy (or one cpupower command, which works with the vendor kernel), takes effect at once, needs no reboot, and can target exactly CPUs 120-127 or all 128.
  REASON: The sysfs path is right: scaling_governor is 0644 root ('-rw-r--r-- 1 root root ... scaling_governor', phoenix-crb-inspection.txt === cpufreq ===), each CPU is its own policy ('related/affected 120: 120 / 120'), the switch takes effect at once (cpufreq_start_governor -> governor->limits -> __cpufreq_driver_target(policy->max) -> cppc_cpufreq_set_target -> cppc_set_perf, no reboot), and no sugov kthread exists to tear down (host: 'count sugov=0', so schedutil runs in fast-switch mode). The cpupower syntax is also valid: cpupower(1) '-c --cpu cpulist' accepts ranges such as '0-3' and commas, and utils/cpufreq-set.c has '/* Default is: set all CPUs */ if (bitmask_isallclear(cpus_chosen)) bitmask_setall(cpus_chosen);'. BUT the 'equivalent' sudo cpupower commands are unsafe on this host: /usr/bin/cpupower (755), /usr/lib/libcpupower.so.1.0.1 (775) and /usr/libexec/cpupower (755) are owned by positron-perf:positron-perf (host 'stat -c %U:%G %a'), installed by the vendor package 'linux-tools 7.1.3-arm-agi-...' (Maintainer 'Custom Kernel Builder <admin@example.com>'); 347 files under /usr /etc /lib /opt /var /boot are owned by uid 1001, including /usr/bin/perf and /usr/sbin/bpftool. Running 'sudo cpupower' executes a binary and a shared library that the unprivileged test account can rewrite, which is a root-escalation path.
  CORRECTION: Use only the sysfs loops (echo performance | sudo tee .../policy$c/scaling_governor) and verify with cat; do not run 'sudo cpupower ...' (nor sudo perf / sudo bpftool) until the admin fixes ownership of the vendor linux-tools files (for example sudo chown -R root:root on every path from 'dpkg -L linux-tools', or reinstall a .deb built with fakeroot). 'scaling_cur_freq -> 8 2800000' after the switch is the request (policy->cur), as the finding says.

[clock-cpufreq/linux-semantics / clock-cpufreq (cppc_cpufreq, schedutil, performance governor, boost, persistence, AMU feedback counters, WFI wake-up, timer slack, thermal, acceptance rule)] C3 -> corrected
  CLAIM: A live governor change survives nothing but is reverted by nothing on this host except a reboot or a human; for persistence, the boot parameter cpufreq.default_governor=performance does apply to the modular cppc_cpufreq driver (the built-in cpufreq core stores the name and applies it when each policy is created at driver probe, and the performance governor is built in), but it is host-wide; a 120-127-only setting needs a small systemd oneshot unit; the packaged cpupower.service (disabled, empty config) is a host-wide no-reboot alternative.
  REASON: Kernel-parameter path confirmed from source: kernel-parameters.txt 'cpufreq.default_governor= [CPU_FREQ] Name of the default cpufreq governor or policy to use. This governor must be registered in the kernel before the cpufreq driver probes.'; cpufreq.c has 'static char default_governor[CPUFREQ_NAME_LEN];' and 'module_param_string(default_governor, default_governor, CPUFREQ_NAME_LEN, 0444);' (hence the 0444 file /sys/module/cpufreq/parameters/default_governor, host read 'schedutil'); cpufreq_init_policy() tries get_governor(policy->last_governor), then get_governor(default_governor), then cpufreq_default_governor(), and ends with 'ret = cpufreq_set_policy(policy, gov, pol);'; cpufreq_core_init() does 'if (!strlen(default_governor)) strscpy(default_governor, gov->name, CPUFREQ_NAME_LEN);'. The performance governor is built in (CONFIG_CPU_FREQ_GOV_PERFORMANCE=y) and cppc_cpufreq is a module with 'MODULE_DEVICE_TABLE(acpi, cppc_acpi_ids);', so the parameter applies at every policy's creation, host-wide. Hotplug does not revert: __cpufreq_offline() does 'strscpy(policy->last_governor, policy->governor->name, CPUFREQ_NAME_LEN);'. The custom oneshot unit is valid under systemd 255: substitution applies only to '${FOO}' and to '$FOO' as a separate word ('Use "${FOO}" as part of a word, or as a word of its own' / 'Use "$FOO" as a separate word on the command line'), and the unit's $(seq ...), $i and $c sit inside one single-quoted word, so they reach /bin/sh unchanged ('To pass a literal dollar sign, use "$$"' is the safer spelling). cpupower.service text confirmed on the host and upstream (cpupower.service.in: Type=oneshot, EnvironmentFile=-/etc/cpupower-service.conf, ExecStart=/usr/libexec/cpupower, RemainAfterExit=yes, WantedBy=multi-user.target, no After= ordering), and /usr/libexec/cpupower runs 'cpupower frequency-set ${GOVERNOR:+ -g "$GOVERNOR"}' on all CPUs. BUT that unit file, the helper script, /usr/bin/cpupower and libcpupower.so are all owned by positron-perf (see C2), so enabling cpupower.service would make root execute files the test account can edit at every boot.
  CORRECTION: Persistence options that stand: (1) host-wide via cpufreq.default_governor=performance on the kernel command line (reboot), (2) CPUs 120-127 only via the custom positron-cpufreq.service oneshot unit (write $$ for each $ or ship the loop as a root-owned script). Drop the cpupower.service option until the admin has chown'ed the vendor linux-tools files to root:root; after that it is a valid host-wide no-reboot path with the ordering caveat the finding states. Also note: the vendor package, not Ubuntu, supplies cpupower.service (Ubuntu's linux-tools does not ship it).
  SOURCE: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/master/Documentation/admin-guide/kernel-parameters.txt (cpufreq.default_governor entry) ; https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/master/drivers/cpufreq/cpufreq.c (default_governor, cpufreq_init_policy, cpufreq_core_init, __cpufreq_offline) ; https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/master/drivers/cpufreq/cppc_cpufreq.c (MODULE_DEVICE_TABLE) ; https://www.freedesktop.org/software/systemd/man/255/systemd.service.html (Command lines) ; https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/master/tools/power/cpupower/cpupower.service.in ; host (this check): systemctl cat cpupower.service; cat /usr/libexec/cpupower; stat -> positron-perf:positron-perf 644 /usr/lib/systemd/system/cpupower.service, 644 /etc/cpupower-service.conf, 755 /usr/libexec/cpupower; cat /sys/module/cpufreq/parameters/default_governor -> schedutil

[clock-cpufreq/linux-semantics / clock-cpufreq (cppc_cpufreq, schedutil, performance governor, boost, persistence, AMU feedback counters, WFI wake-up, timer slack, thermal, acceptance rule)] C5 -> corrected
  CLAIM: Measured on the host now: under schedutil a spinning thread reaches a 2.8 GHz request within about 14 ms and a delivered 2.8 GHz within about 25 ms (est. from 10 ms windows), but a thread with the fake device's sleep-then-spin pattern (about 3 % duty) keeps a 1.6 GHz request and an effective active-time clock of 1.39-1.45 GHz, i.e. 1.9-2.0 times slower than the 2.79 GHz a spinning thread gets.
  REASON: Semantics confirmed: cpufreq_schedutil.c get_next_freq comment 'next_freq = C * max_freq * util / max' with 'Take C = 1.25 for the frequency tipping point at (util / max) = 0.8', and cppc_cpufreq_cpu_init sets 'policy->min = cppc_perf_to_khz(caps, caps->lowest_nonlinear_perf)' = 1,600,000 kHz (lowest_nonlinear_perf = 80), so a low-utilization task is clamped to the 1.6 GHz request. Arithmetic error in the finding: a 3 % duty task maps to 1.25 x 2,800 MHz x 0.03 = 105 MHz (est.), not 'about 170 MHz'; the conclusion (clamped to the 1.6 GHz floor) is unchanged. The measured numbers (14 ms / 25 ms ramp, 1.39-1.45 GHz active-time clock, 2.79 GHz spin) are this check's measurements and cannot be verified from sources; the open question (real post-WFI clock effect vs counter bias) is correctly left open. Note the AMU documentation only states that the counters do not increment in WFI/WFE; it says nothing about the clock in the first microseconds after WFI exit, so the proposed re-measurement under 'performance' is the right discriminator for the floor-frequency hypothesis, and the perf-stat ratio (PMU cycles over scheduler-clock time, user-mode only) is the method less exposed to AMU idle-edge accounting.
  CORRECTION: Replace 'about 170 MHz' with 'about 105 MHz (est.), clamped to the 1,600 MHz policy minimum'.
  SOURCE: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/master/kernel/sched/cpufreq_schedutil.c (get_next_freq) ; https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/master/drivers/cpufreq/cppc_cpufreq.c (policy->min from lowest_nonlinear_perf) ; https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/master/Documentation/arch/arm64/amu.rst ; phoenix-crb-inspection.txt === cpufreq === 'lowest_nonlinear_perf=80 lowest_perf=80'

  CLAIM: The managed NVMe queue interrupts on 120-127 are a strict one-queue-per-CPU mapping (nvme0/nvme1 queue_count=129 = 1 admin + 128 I/O queues; /sys/block/nvme0n1/mq has 128 hardware queues; hctx 118..125 map to CPUs 120..127; IRQ nvme0q119..126 and nvme1q119..126 have smp_affinity exactly {120}..{127}). Therefore a queue IRQ on CPU N fires only for I/O submitted from CPU N (blk-mq sends a request to the hardware queue of the submitting CPU, and that queue's vector is affined to that CPU). Writes to /proc/irq/N/smp_affinity for a managed IRQ are refused by the kernel (irq_can_set_affinity_usr() is false for managed IRQs; v6.8 returns EIO 'Input/output error', current mainline returns EPERM; the vendor 7.1.3 errno was not determined). 'isolcpus=managed_irq' has no effect on this host because every such mask contains only one (isolated) CPU; the kernel documentation says exactly this.
  REASON: Host facts confirmed at 01:14 UTC: 'nvme0 queue_count=129', 'nvme1 queue_count=129', 'nvme0n1 mq dirs: 128', 'hctx118:120 hctx119:121 ... hctx125:127' (also hctx0:1, hctx1:3, hctx126:2, hctx127:0, so the mapping is not simply k+2 everywhere), 'irq 176 smp_affinity_list=120 effective=120 actions=nvme1q119 chip=MBI-PCI-MSIX-000b:01:00.0', same pattern for 183/304/311, and the file's 16 vectors touching 120-127.   CLAIM: TLB shootdown on this arm64 host is not an IPI and cannot be isolated away: the kernel invalidates remote TLBs with broadcast TLBI instructions in hardware, and the 'Broken broadcast TLBI completion' feature in dmesg is the CPU erratum of CVE-2026-53354 (Arm CVE-2025-10263), mitigated by ARM64_WORKAROUND_REPEAT_TLBI (the sequence TLBI;DSB is issued twice); no IPI-based TLB flush code is present in the running kernel. The cost to an isolated CPU is snoop traffic from any process on the host that unmaps or remaps memory, plus shared L3 (128 MiB, shared by all 128 CPUs) and DRAM bandwidth; the only mitigation is to keep the rest of the host idle during a run.
  REASON: The dmesg line '[ 0.000000] CPU features: detected: Broken broadcast TLBI completion' is in the followup file. In mainline arch/arm64/kernel/cpu_errata.c the entry with '.desc = "Broken broadcast TLBI completion"' has '.capability = ARM64_WORKAROUND_REPEAT_TLBI_SYNC' (under CONFIG_ARM64_WORKAROUND_REPEAT_TLBI_SYNC) and '.match_list = arm64_repeat_tlbi_list', whose CONFIG_ARM64_ERRATUM_4118414 block lists Neoverse N1/N2/V1/V2/V3/V3AE, Cortex-A76/A77/A78 family, A710, X1-X925, NVIDIA Olympus and Azure Cobalt 100; so the capability name in the finding (ARM64_WORKAROUND_REPEAT_TLBI) is the older/sibling name, and the erratum number for this CPU family is 4118414. The CVE mapping is consistent with the web record for CVE-2026-53354 (fix 'arm64: errata: Mitigate TLBI errata on various Arm CPUs'; hardware issue CVE-2025-10263; 'a broadcast TLBI;DSB sequence may complete before the global observation of writes'; mitigation = an additional TLBI;DSB). The host's kallsyms has arm64_repeat_tlbi_list and no symbol matching ipi_flush_tlb/flush_tlb_ipi (0 matches), consistent with arm64's hardware-broadcast TLB maintenance. 'index3 L3 Unified 131072K shared=0-127' is in === topology_120_127 ===. The vendor tree was not read (medium confidence stands).
  CORRECTION: The dmesg feature 'Broken broadcast TLBI completion' is the ARM64_WORKAROUND_REPEAT_TLBI_SYNC capability (match list arm64_repeat_tlbi_list, Arm erratum 4118414 for Neoverse V3/V3AE and related cores; the fix commit is CVE-2026-53354, the hardware issue CVE-2025-10263); its mitigation repeats the TLBI;DSB sequence. TLB invalidation on this arm64 host is hardware broadcast, not an IPI, so no isolation setting removes it.

  CLAIM: A busy CPU on the running kernel takes 252-253 arch_timer interrupts per second (the HZ=250 scheduler tick; re-measured 1264 and 1265 in 5 s on CPU 121 at 00:48 UTC, 1261 and 1258 in round3), and only nohz_full can reduce that (to the residual 1 Hz tick plus the task's own timers). On this kernel 'nohz_full=' IS parsed (its handler housekeeping_nohz_full_setup exists in /proc/kallsyms) but is rejected at runtime with the dmesg warning 'Housekeeping: nohz unsupported. Build with CONFIG_NO_HZ_FULL', and 'rcu_nocbs=' has no handler at all (rcu_nocb_setup absent), so it would be logged as an unknown parameter; neither has any effect. Draft S1's 'reported as unknown parameters and ignored' is therefore right in effect for both nohz_full= and rcu_nocbs=; only S1's reason (no handler) is wrong for nohz_full=. The same return-0 path applies to a misspelled isolcpus flag ('isolcpus: Invalid flag %.*s' then return 0), so the post-reboot dmesg check must also grep 'Invalid flag'. (2) 'only nohz_full can reduce that (to the residual 1 Hz tick plus the task's own timers)': on current kernels the residual 1 Hz tick of a nohz_full CPU is a remote tick run on a housekeeping CPU (kernel/sched/core.c sched_tick_start(): 'if (housekeeping_cpu(cpu, HK_TYPE_KERNEL_NOISE)) return;' then 'queue_delayed_work(system_dfl_wq, &twork->work, HZ);'; cpu-isolation.rst: 'Housekeeping CPUs must run a 1Hz residual remote scheduler tick on behalf of the isolated CPUs.'). A nohz_full pass CPU running one user task with no tick dependency receives zero tick interrupts; what remains on it are its own hrtimers and tick dependencies (POSIX CPU timers, perf sampling, a second runnable task).

  CLAIM: On this kernel a busy CPU receives a function-call IPI for every expedited RCU grace period on the whole host, and such grace periods are triggered by ordinary service activity (a sandboxed systemd service start/stop sets up and tears down a mount namespace): measured on CPU 121 during a 5 s pinned spin, 0 IPIs with nothing else started, 25 IPIs when timedatectl activated systemd-timedated three times, 2 IPIs while idle. isolcpus does not remove this; only nohz_full does, because a task on a nohz_full CPU runs in an extended quiescent state (EQS, 'the kernel need not interrupt it for RCU') and the expedited code skips CPUs in EQS.
  REASON: Mechanism confirmed in source: kernel/rcu/tree_exp.h __sync_rcu_exp_select_node_cpus() takes 'snap = ct_rcu_watching_cpu_acquire(cpu); if (rcu_watching_snap_in_eqs(snap)) mask_ofl_test |= mask;' and IPIs the rest with 'ret = smp_call_function_single(cpu, rcu_exp_handler, NULL, 0);'; fs/namespace.c namespace_unlock() does 'if (likely(hlist_empty(&head))) return; synchronize_rcu_expedited();' (mount teardown). On this kernel user mode is not an extended quiescent state (no CONFIG_CONTEXT_TRACKING_USER, 'context_tracking_enabled' absent from kallsyms), so a busy CPU is IPI'd and an idle one is skipped. Replicated: 4 s spin on CPU 125 with 3x timedatectl that started systemd-timedated (ExecMainStartTimestamp 01:20:16 UTC, PrivateTmp=yes ProtectSystem=strict ProtectHome=yes) -> IPI1=26; control -> 0; idle CPU with the same calls -> 2. Two corrections to the attribution, and one large omission (see extra findings: /sys/kernel/rcu_normal).
  CORRECTION: The IPIs come from the sandboxed service's start (its mount-namespace setup/teardown), not from D-Bus or status queries: with systemd-timedated already running, 3x timedatectl during a 4 s spin gave IPI1=0 (test A), and 3x 'systemctl is-active apt-daily.timer' plus one 'ps -eLo' plus one 'cat /proc/interrupts' gave IPI1=0 (test B). So 'every ssh login' and 'every systemd service start' are not established; the established trigger is any service start or stop that creates or tears down a mount namespace (every sandboxed unit: fwupd-refresh, man-db, systemd-timedated, ...). The service's later idle-exit teardown was not measured. The rate quoted for 23:47-00:42 UTC (2.5 IPI1/s over 8 CPUs) includes the inspections' own service activations. And the implication 'a concrete item that only the rebuilt nohz_full kernel removes' is wrong: writing 1 to /sys/kernel/rcu_normal (root, live, reversible; mode 0644 on the host, current value 0) or booting with rcupdate.rcu_normal=1 makes synchronize_rcu_expedited() fall back to a normal grace period ('if (rcu_gp_is_normal()) { synchronize_rcu_normal(); return; }'), which removes these IPIs on the current kernel.

  CLAIM: The managed NVMe queue interrupts on 120-127 are a strict one-queue-per-CPU mapping (nvme0/nvme1 queue_count=129 = 1 admin + 128 I/O queues; /sys/block/nvme0n1/mq has 128 hardware queues; hctx 118..125 map to CPUs 120..127; IRQ nvme0q119..126 and nvme1q119..126 have smp_affinity exactly {120}..{127}). Therefore a queue IRQ on CPU N fires only for I/O submitted from CPU N (blk-mq sends a request to the hardware queue of the submitting CPU, and that queue's vector is affined to that CPU). Writes to /proc/irq/N/smp_affinity for a managed IRQ are refused by the kernel (irq_can_set_affinity_usr() is false for managed IRQs; v6.8 returns EIO 'Input/output error', current mainline returns EPERM; the vendor 7.1.3 errno was not determined). 'isolcpus=managed_irq' has no effect on this host because every such mask contains only one (isolated) CPU; the kernel documentation says exactly this.
  REASON: Re-verified on the host: nvme0 and nvme1 queue_count=129; /sys/block/nvme0n1/mq hctx118..125 cpu_list = 120..127 one CPU each; IRQs 176 (nvme1q119) and 304 (nvme0q119) smp_affinity_list=120 effective=120, IRQs 183/311 (q126) = 127, all /proc/irq/N/smp_affinity_list mode 0444. kernel/irq/manage.c irq_can_set_affinity_usr(): 'return __irq_can_set_affinity(desc) && !irqd_affinity_is_managed(&desc->irq_data);'; irq_do_set_affinity() managed block: 'if (irqd_affinity_is_managed(data) && housekeeping_enabled(HK_TYPE_MANAGED_IRQ))' ... 'cpumask_and(tmp_mask, mask, hk_mask); if (!cpumask_intersects(tmp_mask, cpu_online_mask)) prog_mask = mask;' (requested mask kept when it has no housekeeping CPU). kernel-parameters.txt managed_irq text as quoted ('best effort', 'no effect on the interrupt routing decision', 'only delivered when tasks running on those isolated CPUs submit IO'). blk-mq picks the hardware queue from the submitting CPU, so the one-queue-per-CPU statement holds.
  CORRECTION: The open errno question is settled: kernel/irq/proc.c at tag v7.1 has 'if (!irq_can_set_affinity_usr(irq) || no_irq_affinity) return -EPERM;' (v6.8: 'return -EIO;'), so on the 7.1.3 kernel the safe root test should print 'Operation not permitted'.   CLAIM: TLB shootdown on this arm64 host is not an IPI and cannot be isolated away: the kernel invalidates remote TLBs with broadcast TLBI instructions in hardware, and the 'Broken broadcast TLBI completion' feature in dmesg is the CPU erratum of CVE-2026-53354 (Arm CVE-2025-10263), mitigated by ARM64_WORKAROUND_REPEAT_TLBI (the sequence TLBI;DSB is issued twice); no IPI-based TLB flush code is present in the running kernel. The cost to an isolated CPU is snoop traffic from any process on the host that unmaps or remaps memory, plus shared L3 (128 MiB, shared by all 128 CPUs) and DRAM bandwidth; the only mitigation is to keep the rest of the host idle during a run.
  REASON: The core semantic is right: arm64 invalidates remote TLBs with broadcast TLBI instructions, not IPIs (no ipi_flush_tlb*/flush_tlb_ipi* symbol in the host kallsyms; absence of a symbol is weak evidence, but it agrees with the architecture). Host MIDR 0x410fd832 = Arm part 0xd83 variant 0 revision 2 = Neoverse V3AE r0p2 (lscpu 'Stepping: r0p2'), which is in the erratum list. The CVE pair (CVE-2026-53354 kernel-side, Arm CVE-2025-10263 hardware) and the fix title 'arm64: errata: Mitigate TLBI errata on various Arm CPUs' are confirmed by Red Hat Bugzilla 2495939 and the CVE trackers; the mitigation is an additional TLBI;DSB after each affected sequence. (shadow passwd.c pw_status: 'if (strprefix(pass, "*") || strprefix(pass, "!"))' -> "L"). sshd's own locked-account refusal is skipped because the host has 'UsePAM yes' (sshd_config line 94, host read): OpenSSH 9.6p1 auth.c allowed_user() reads 'if (!options.use_pam && platform_locked_account(pw))'. So key logins work (101 sessions observed) and a password attempt is rejected by PAM against the '!'-prefixed hash. (2) The proposed drop-in is valid on this OpenSSH (1:9.6p1-3ubuntu13.20, host dpkg-query): servconf.c saves and restores the Match state around every included file ('Don't let included files clobber the containing file's Match state', 'oactive = *activep;' ... '*activep = oactive;'), and sshd_config(5) lists PasswordAuthentication among the keywords allowed after Match. Note for any host-wide hardening instead: 'PasswordAuthentication yes' is set in four places (sshd_config line 66 and all three drop-ins 01-arm-svk-password-auth.conf, 60-cloudimg-settings.conf, 99-arm-svk.conf, host read) and sshd uses the first obtained value, so a host-wide 'no' would need a drop-in that sorts before 01-.
  CORRECTION: Password authentication is offered to positron-perf but cannot succeed: the account's password hash is locked ('L' = hash begins with '!' or '*'), sshd skips its own lock check under UsePAM yes and PAM rejects any password against the locked hash, while public-key login proceeds. The optional drop-in /etc/ssh/sshd_config.d/10-positron-perf.conf with 'Match User positron-perf' / 'PasswordAuthentication no' is syntactically safe on OpenSSH 9.6p1 (Match state does not leak out of an included file); run 'sudo sshd -t' before 'sudo systemctl reload ssh'.
